### 955. Ezek. 44:9

### 955.Ezek. 44'9(2_50)(1) trans

Jonathan Edwards1749.

______________________________________________________

Ezekiel 44. 9. Thus saith the Lord no

stranger uncircumcised in Heart nor un-

circumcised in Flesh shall enter into

my sanctuary of any stranger that is

among the Chil. of Israel.

This latter Part of the Prophecy of

Ezekiel is by all allowed to be a Prophecy

of the state of the Chh in Gospel Times

And here in this Chapter together with

the three four Preceding Chapters the

Gosp. state of the Gospel Chh which

should be set up after X came, is repre-

sented by a by a vision the Prophet

had of a very beautiful & magnificent

Temple or sanctuary . and here in

this Chap. when the description of this

Glorious Temple represente

Gospel Temple or Chh is finished

Then it is declared who shall & who

shall not be admitted into this sanctuary

or Chh. viz. only Those who are Cir-

cumcised in H. & circumcised & the Flesh.[^007-note1]

to partake in the ordinances of the House

____

The People and Priests are blamed that

formerly during the continuance of the

Old Testament sanctuary & Temple they

had taken no more care as to this matter

but had admitted into the Chh & sanctuary

[-] strangers uncircumcised in Heart & uncir

cumcised in Flesh to partake when they

offered those ordinances God's Bread the

Fat & the Blood . as in the two verses

next preceding the Text -------------

______________ ___________________

-------- Here They were are blamed that

They admitted such as were uncircum-

cised in H. & uncir in Flesh into Gods sanc

tuary to partake of Gods bread the Fat &

the Blood .

i. e. to

partake

of those

ordinanc

sacrifices

and ordi

nances

wherein the Bread of G Body & Blood[^007-note2]

of X were represented .

This G. manifests his great dislike of

calls it an abomination & foretells in the

Text that more special days of Care shall

be taken to prevent it in the days of the

Gospel. when the

& here are two Qualificaitons insisted

on 1. That such as are admitted

be un circumcised in Flesh i. e that

they shall be the subjects of that external

ordinance that should come in the Room

of circumcision viz Baptism.

2. That not only so but that they

should be circumcised in Heart i. e

by by which we are to understand that

They should be the subjects of that inward

change of Heart that was typified by

circumcision

not that

all should

be really

so

i. e they

should be

so in pro[fe]-

ssion & in an Eye of Charity or in[^007-note3]

the Eye of a Xtian Judgmt

______________________________________________

doc . Tis the mind & will of G.

that none should be admitted to full Com-

munion in the Chh of X but such as

in Profession & in the Eye of a Xti rati

reasonable Judgmt truly converted saints

Persons or godly Persons.

____________

This doc is undeniably evident from

the words of the Text . strangers are

not to be admitted i. e those who are

alienes & strangers from the common wealth

of Israel. tis expressly said that no

stranger uncircumcised in Heart shall be

admitted. the contrary is spoken of as an

Abomination . i. e to admit such

as are not visibly circumcised in Heart

& dont profess or pretend to it .

Mr. Stoddard Hims. allows that they

should be visibly circumcised in Heart

Reference to the Lords supper. to which

I would humbly answer if it has no

particular Reference to the Lords supper yet[^007-note4]

it plainly & evidently has a Reference

to a being admitted into the Xtian Chh. &

so by Conseq. has has a Reference to a

being admitted to the ordinances of the

Chh. It says no stranger uncircumcised

in Heart shall be enter into Gods sanctuary

i. e that sanctuary or Temple that

Ezekiel had a vision of & had been

describing just before & that is not the

old Testament Temple built of Timber

& stones but the new T. Temple

i. e the Christian Chh. & this is not

a thing that I know of in controversy

that I know of it being the current

or a Thing that [--] orthodox divines &

Xtians do doubt of . [--] tis But tis

plain that the N. T. Temple or sanctuary

or Temple or House of G. is his Chh.

The SS. is plain & abundant in this matter

1 Tim. 3. 15. That Thou mayst know

how Thou oughtest to behave thy self in

the House of G. which is the Chh of the living[^007-note5]

G. 1 Pet. 2. 5. ye also as lively

stones are built up a sp. House

Eph. 2. 20, 21 ye are built on the Founda.

of the Proph. & apostles J. X Hims. being

the Chief Corner stone in whom all the

Building fitly framed together & compacted

groweth unto an holy Temple to the L.

& the same & it might be shewn from

innumerable other Places that the N. T.

Temple is the Xtian Chh. & theref.

when X the Prophet foretells that in the

days of the Gosp. none shall be admitted

into the Temple but that the Priests or

ministers of the Gospel shall admit none

into the Temple but those that are visibly

circumcised in H. the meaning must be that

they should

admit

none

but such as in profession and visibility[^007-note6]

are converted.

for by circumcision is of H. in [-] is

meant conversion as is Evident by

Rom. 2. 28. 29. He is not a Jew which

is one outwardly neither is that circumci-

sion which is outward in the Flesh but

He is a Jew which is one inwardly and

Circumcision is that of the Heart in the

sp. & not in the Letter whose Praise is not

of men but of G. Tis Evident here

& [-------] in many other Places that might

be mentioned that the [--] by Circum-

cision of Heart is here meant Conversion

of Heart & not moral sincerity.

so that the doctrine I insist upon is a

doctrine very plainly & undeniably taught

in the words of the Text . They are

words of

the most

high where

in He has

plainly told

us his mind.

& strictly forbidden the Contrary . & told[^007-note7]

as are not visibly & in such into the sanctuary

Before I proceed to the more particular Proof of[^007-note8]

the doctrine I would say in a few words what

I intend in it when I say comm

when I say communicants ought to be in the

Eye of a reasonable Judgment Godly Persons I mean

not a private but a publick Cha Judgement

Tis properly a visibility of Godliness to the Eye of

a publick Charity & not a private Judgment that

gives a Person a Right to be received as a visi-

ble Xtian by the publick society . The minis

ter in this affair is not to act as a publick officer

in behalf of the Chh & not therefore is to accept

& not [--] as a private Person in his own Name

and therefore in this matter He must not be

determined by any skill He may think He

has to discern & search mens Hearts . But as

the Person & see to it that He has a good doc

trinal knowledge and therefore He must ac-

cept the serious Profession the Person makes of Godli-

struct the Person & to see to it that He has a good

OBJECTIONS ANSWERED[^009-note69]

Before I proceed to Answer particular

Objections I would Lay down first

lay down this

POSITION as a maxim of undoubted

Truth [-] which is this

That Argument which is brought to sup-

port one scheme against another can be

of no avail that is much against the

scheme it would support as that which

it would destroy

It is an old and approved maxim

that that argument which proves too

much proves nothing i.e if if it proves

too much for Him that brings proves as much

against Hims. as it does against his opponent

it is an evidence that it pu nothing to his

This Purpose or to help his Cause.

I have heard that some have said that is

not a fair way of answering Objections to

ans go about to shew that there is They

are as much against the Cause of the Objec

tor as against the Objectors own scheme

as against the scheme he objects against

But certainly that is a fair way of answering[^009-note70]

an Objection & sufficient Answer to the

Objection of an opponent that shews that

the Objection is nothing to his Purpose As

an Objection cannot be that is objection

against himself [-] & destroys the very thing

that He would support

As for Is Instance Let us suppose some stranger

Animal to be found in the woods some thing in

human shape and a great dispute should arise

between two men concerning it whether it

were a man or a Beast Beast[-] and He

that would prove that it is a Beast & not

a man should be insis insist on this Argu-

ment to prove it viz. that it was no living

Creature at all had no Bodily senses

& was no living Creature & therefore should

thence argue that it must needs be a

Brute & not a man His Argument would

overthrow Himself as much as his

be as much against Hims. as his opponent

for if it is no animal at all tis certainly

tis not a Beast & theref. his arguement

objection overthrows Him & therefore avails

nothing at all to help his Cause & so is nothing

to the Purpose as that dispute which he has

with his opponent

The Business of a dispute is to maintain[^009-note71]

one Cause against another and therefore if

He that pretends to do that don't do it he

does nothing. and if he brings arguments

in the dispute and his opponent shews

that his argument plainly that his argumt

dont help his Cause becasue it is as much

against his own Cause

is shews his as much against his own Cause as

the other tis a it fully makes void his

argument because it this demonstrates

that his argument dont help his Cause

and if it be proved that it dont help his

Cause it is proved that it is nothing to th

his Purpose & if this be proved then

the Objection is vacated wholy vacated

& made void

If a man brings an Argument to maintain

one scheme in oppositi against

If one in a dispute brings an Argument

that force of which lies equally against his own

Cause as the other & yet will stand to it

that his argument he overthrows Hims.

his maintaining his he must either shew that

his argument is not so much against hims.

as the other or es else He must give up

his argument for if He he owns that

the argument is as much against Hims.

as his opponent & yet stands to it that his

argument is good & conclusive He in Effect[^009-note72]

gives up herein gives up his own Cause for

he brings one a he hims. brings an argument that

maintain he allows to be conclusive & yet

owns that it is against Hims. & therefore in effect

If an opponent should acknowledge that

his Objection If one sho

If my opponent in a dispute should

acknowledge that the argumt he brings is

equally h against Hims. as against me but

yet should tell me that it is not a fair way

of Arguing for me to make such a Reply

then I may in my turn take his own

argument & use it against Him & he allows

it to be a good argu argument & he has

nothing to answer He is condemned out

of his own mouth & He must not tell

me that the argument is as much against

me & him & He hims. says that is no

fair way of arguing . so that hereby He

supl supplants Hims & gives up his own

Cause In such a Case for him to hold

fast his own objection is to hold fast his

own overthrow & by insisting that his object

objection is strong He in Effect insists that

his own Cause is weak & its overthrow sure

Having obs laid down this maxim

which I desire to be born in mind I now pro-

ceed to answer particular Objections

future Prosperity of this the Town is that

you should watch against the Encroachmts

posed . The Elder People may

### 965. John 14:27

### 965.John 14'27 (8_50) trans

Jonathan EdwardsSERMON XII.

The Peace Which CHRIST gives his true

Followers.

JOHN, xiv. 27.

Peace I leave with you, my Peace I give unto you: not

as the World giveth, give I unto you.

THESE words are a part of a most affectionate

and affecting discourse that CHRIST had with

his disciples, the same evening in which he was be-

tray'd; knowing that he was to be crucified the next day.

This discourse begins with the 31st verse of the xiii

chapter, and is continued to the end of the xvi chap-

ter. CHRIST began his discourse, after he had par-

took of the passover with them, after he had institu-

ted and administered the sacrament of the supper, and

after Judas was gone out, and none were left but his

true and faithful disciples; whom he now addresses as

his dear children. This was the last discourse that

ever CHRIST had with them before his death. As it

was his parting discourse, and as it were his dying dis-

course, so it is on many accounts the most remarka-

ble of all the discourses of CHRIST, which we have

recorded in our bibles. [^020-note1]

IT is evident this discourse made a deep impression

on the minds of the disciples; and we may suppose

that it did so, in a special manner, on the mind of

John the beloved disciple, whose heart was especially

full of love to him, and who had just then been lean-

ing on his bosom. In this discourse CHRIST had told

his dear disciples that he was going away, which fil-

led them with sorrow and heaviness. The words of

the text are some of the words which CHRIST said to

comfort them, and to relieve their sorrow. He sup-

ports them with the promise of that peace which he

would leave with them, and which they would have

in him and with him, when he was gone.

THIS promise he delivers in three emphatical ex-

pressions which illustrate one another. "Peace I leave

with you." As much as to say, though I am going

away, yet I will not take all comfort away with me.

While I have been with you, I have been your support

and comfort, and you have had peace in me, in the midst

of the losses you have sustained and troubles you

have met with in this evil generation. This peace I

will not take from you, but leave it with you with

great advantage, and in a more full possession.

"My peace I give unto you." CHRIST by calling

it his peace signifies two things.

1. THAT it was his own, that which he had to

give. It was the peculiar benefit that he had to be-

stow on his children; now he was about to die and

leave the world as to his human presence. Silver

and gold he had none: for while in his estate of hu-

miliation he was poor. The foxes had holes, and the

birds of the air had nests: but the son of man had

not where to lay his head. Luke ix. 58. He had

no earthly estate to leave to his disciples who were as

it were his family: but he had peace to give them. [^020-note2]

2. IT was his peace that he gave them; as it was

the same kind of peace which he himself enjoyed. The

same excellent and divine peace which he ever had in

GOD; and which he was about to receive in his exal-

ted state in a vastly greater perfection and fullness: for

the happiness CHRIST gives to his people, is a partici-

pation of his own happiness: agreeable to what CHRIST

says in this same dying discourse of his, chapter

xv. 11[.] "These things have I said unto you that my

"joy might remain in you." And in his prayer that he

made with his disciples at the conclusion of this dis-

course. Chapter xvii. 13. "And now come I un-

"to thee, and these things I speak in the world, that

"they might have my joy fulfilled in themselves."

And verse 22. "And the glory which thou gavest

"me, I have given them."

CHRIST here alludes to men's making their wills be-

fore death. When parents are about to leave their

children by death, they are wont in their last will and

testament to give them their estate. That estate which

they themselves were wont to possess and enjoy. So

it was with CHRIST when he was about to leave the

world, with respect to the peace which he gave his

disciples; only with this difference, that earthly pa-

rents, when they

die tho They leave the same Estates which[^020-note3]

to their Chil. which They have Thems. enjoyed

heretofore enjoyed yet when the Chil.

come to the full Possession of it They Parents

enjoy it no more the Parents cant enjoy

it with their Chil. whereas X with Re-

spect to th Xs Peace He did not only Possess

it before Hims. before his death when He

left it bequeathed to his d. but also

afterwards more fully so that They were

received to possess it with Him.

which is as much as to say my Gifts & Lega-

like those of the Rich which the Rich & great

men of the O are wont to Leave to their

Heirs when they die . they what they

They bequeath to their Chil. their Oly Pos-

But the thing that I give you is my

Peace a vastly different thing what they

have to give & which cant be obtained

by all that they can bestow or their

Chil. Inherit from Them

given forth by X in all the Time that He

upon earth, as in this discourse. CHRIST pro-

mises them mansions in his fathers house chapter

xiv. 1, 2, 3. Here he promises them whatever bles-

sings they should need and ask in his name. Chapter

xv. 7. xvi. 23, 24. Here he does more solemnly and

fully than any where else, give forth and confirm the

promise of the HOLY SPIRIT, which is the sum of the [^020-note7]

blessings of the covenant of grace. Chap. xiv. 16.---

xvii. 26.-- xv. 25.--- xvi. 7. Here he promises them

his own and his father's gracious presence and favor.

Chapter xiv. 18. --- xix. 20, 21. Here he promises them

peace in the text. Here he promises them his joy.

Chapter xv. 11. Here he promises grace to bring

forth holy fruits---Chapter xv. 5. And victory over

the world. Chapter xvi. 33. And indeed there seems

to be no where else so full and compleat an edition of

the covenant of grace in the whole bible, as in this

dying discourse of CHRIST with his eleven true disci-

ples.

THIS covenant between CHRIST and his children is

like a will or testament also in this respect, that it be-

comes effectual, and a way is made for putting it in

execution no other way than by his death; as the a-

postle observes it is with a will or testament among

men. For a testament is of force after men are dead.

Heb. ix. 17. For though the covenant of grace in-

deed was of force before the death of CHRIST, yet it

was of force no other wise than by his death: so that

his death then did virtually intervene; being already

undertaken and engaged. As a man's heirs come by

the legacies bequeathed to them, no otherwise than

by the death of the testator, so men come by the spi-

ritual and eternal inheritance, no otherwise than by

the death of CHRIST. If it had not been for the death

of CHRIST they never could have obtained it.

II. A GREAT blessing that CHRIST in his testament

hath bequeathed to his true followers is his peace.

Here are two things that I would observe particular-

ly, viz. That CHRIST hath bequeathed to believers

true peace; and then that the peace he has given them

is his peace.

1. OUR LORD JESUS CHRIST has bequeathed true [^020-note8]

peace and comfort to his followers. CHRIST is called

the prince of peace. Isai ix. 6. And when he was

born into the world, the angels on that joyful and

wonderful occasion sang glory to GOD in the highest,

on earth peace; because of that peace which he should

procure for and bestow on the children of men;

Peace with GOD, and peace one with another, and

tranquility and peace within themselves: which last

is especially the benefit spoken of in the text. This

CHRIST has procured for his followers and laid a

foundation for their enjoyment of, in that he has pro-

cured for them the other two, viz, peace with GOD,

and one with another. He has procured for them

peace and reconciliation with GOD and his favor and

friendship; in that he satisfied for their sins, and laid

a foundation for the perfect removal of the guilt of

sin, and the forgiveness of all their trespasses, and

wrought out for them a perfect and glorious righte-

ousness, most acceptable to GOD and sufficient to re-

commend them to GOD'S full acceptance and to the

adoption of children, and to the eternal fruits of his

fatherly kindness.
